## Page 1


A TREE–FREE APPROACH TO 3D YANG–MILLS LANGEVIN
DYNAMIC.
ANALYTIC ESTIMATES AND THE EXISTENCE OF A MODEL
FOR A REGULARITY STRUCTURE
A. SEVOSTYANOV
Abstract. Using the multi–index approach to regularity structures due to F.
Otto et al., we construct a regularity structure and a model for it associated to
the stochastic Langevin equation for the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills functional.
For the model we also obtain global stochastic and global pointwise weighted
Besov type estimates which hold almost surely. The model is defined as a limit
of a sequence of smooth models introduced with the help of a mollified noise.
When the mollification is removed the sequence converges in a certain topology
defined with the help of the stochastic estimates. To obtain these results we
develop the multi–index approach for systems of equations with vector–valued
white noises. This project is motivated by the problem for constructing 3D
Euclidean Yang–Mills measure and by the earlier results of the author on
the related problem of canonical quantization of the Yang–Mills field on the
Minkowski space.

1. Introduction
1.1. Canonical quantization of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski
space and 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure. It is well known that the Yang–
Mills theory on the Minkowski space is the main ingredient of the most complete
contemporary model in quantum field theory, called the Standard Model, which
describes weak, strong and electromagnetic interactions of elementary particles.
The problem for defining a mathematically rigorous quantum Yang–Mills theory
remains open for many decades, and became central in quantum field theory at least
since the Standard Model was introduced in the end of 1960s. One of the aspects
of this problem, a rigorous definition of the quantized Yang–Mills Hamiltonian and
the proof of the mass gap property for it, is stated as a Clay Mathematical Institute
Millennium problem (see [31]).
In paper [43] it was shown that a natural candidate for the canonically quan-
tized Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field on the Minkowski space is the Orn-
stein–Uhlenbeck operator associated to the yet to be defined 3D Euclidean Yang–
Mills measure (see [21], Chapter 3 for the Hamiltonian formulation for the Yang–
Mills field on the Minkowski space).
Actually, in [43] it was observed that the classical Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills
field on the trivial K–bundle over the Minkowski space associated to the adjoint
representation of a compact Lie group K with Lie algebra k belongs to a class of
Hamiltonians which can be described in the toy finite–dimensional case as follows.

The function ψ is a quantization parameter which can be chosen arbitrary if we
do not require that the quantized components of the momentum in the Hamilton-
ian formalism should be self–adjoint in L2(M, dµ). This restriction can be often
lifted in quantum field theory when M is infinite–dimensional and the Hamiltonian
formalism momentum p has no physical meaning, hence its quantized components
do not need to be self–adjoint (do not confuse p with the relativistic momentum
which is, of course, a physical observable).
On the other hand, the possibility of introducing an extra factor ψ in the def-
inition of the measure dµ becomes primarily important in quantum field theory
because the usual Lebesgue measure does not exist on infinite–dimensional spaces,
but choosing ψ appropriately one can expect to find proper probabilistic counter-
parts of measures of the type dµ = ψe−2ϕdx which can be defined on such spaces.
The key observation of [43] is that the Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field on
the Minkowski space is of type (1.1.1), where M is the space of gauge equivalence
classes of connections A on the trivial K–bundle over R3 associated to the adjoint
representation of K, and ϕ is the Chern–Simons functional which we denote by
CS. A natural metric on M is induced by the natural metric ⟨·, ·⟩on the space of
k-valued differential one–forms on R3, see formula (1.2.1) below.
The discussion in this introduction is rather informal, and we do not rigorously
define the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections on R3 required for quan-
tization. This is a subtle matter as probabilistic measures on infinite–dimensional
function spaces are typically supported on spaces which consist of very singular
generalized functions (see [10, 11] for a rigorous definition of such spaces in the
case of gauge equivalence classes of connections on a two–dimensional or a three–
dimensional torus).
A natural choice of the function ψ for which the measure dµ is expected to exist
in the case of the Yang–Mills field is such that

A. SEVOSTYANOV
where Y M(A) is the Euclidean Yang–Mills functional on R3 (see formula (1.2.4)
below), m > 0 is a parameter, and we identify the affine space of connections with
the space of k–valued one–forms on R3 by fixing an origin Z, so that the expression
⟨(A −Z), (A −Z)⟩is gauge invariant1.
As explained in [43], the necessity for introducing a non–zero parameter m in the
right hand side of formula (1.1.2) for the “density function” of the corresponding
conjectural measure dµ, called the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure, is already
clear in the abelian case when K = U(1), and in this case the corresponding
measure can be constructed by classical methods of functional analysis. In fact,
for K = U(1) the expression in the exponent in the right hand side of (1.1.2) is
quadratic in A, and the gauge action is reduced to adding differentials of functions,
so that one can fix a “gauge” (e.g the Coulomb gauge as in [43]) which provides a
model for the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections, and make the 3D
Euclidean Yang–Mills measure manifestly Gaussian on this model. Note that the
corresponding quantization of the Yang–Mills Hamiltonian in this case, given by
the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck operator associated to this Gaussian measure, is different
from that used in quantum electrodynamics.
As shown in [43], this operator exhibits one important property which is also ex-
pected from the quantized self–adjoint Yang–Mills Hamiltonian in the non–abelian
case and which is primarily important for applications in quantum chromodynam-
ics: this quantized Hamiltonian has a gap in the spectrum which separates the zero
eigenvalue corresponding to the ground state from the rest of the spectrum.
As it is clear from the preceding discussion the problem for quantizing of the
classical Hamiltonian of the Yang–Mills field defined on the Minkowski space is
now reduced to the problem for defining the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure. In
the next part of the Introduction we shall discuss this problem in more detail.
1.2. 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills measure and the Langevin equation asso-
ciated to the 3D Euclidean Yang–Mills functional. Firstly, we recall some
basic facts from gauge theory. Let K be a compact semi-simple Lie group, k its
Lie algebra and g the complexification of k. We denote by (·, ·) the Killing form
of g. Recall that the restriction of this form to k is non-degenerate and negatively
defined. For the Lie bracket [·, ·] on k it will be important to consider a family of Lie
brackets g[·, ·] on the vector space k, and the corresponding family of Lie brackets
on g, parametrized by g ∈R which is called the coupling constant. We shall denote
the vector space k equipped with the Lie bracket corresponding to g by kg, and the
corresponding compact semi-simple Lie group by Kg.
We shall consider the Yang–Mills functional on the affine space of smooth connec-
tions on the trivial Kg-bundle over the standard Euclidean space R3 and associated
to the adjoint representation of Kg. Fixing a trivialization of this bundle and the
trivial connection as an origin in the affine space of connections we can identify
this space with the space Ω1(R3, kg) of kg-valued 1-forms on R3. Let A be such a
connection.
1In [43] a slightly different choice of ψ is used. The choice of ψ as in (1.1.2) is more natural
and symmetric, especially in the case of non–abelian K.

2.
To circumvent this obstruction a more deep modification of equation (1.2.8) is re-
quired which is achieved by adding to the right hand side extra terms, called counter
terms, depending on ρ. A proper realization of this idea initiated by M. Hairer in
pioneering paper [27] for a class of non-linear stochastic differential equations and
developed in [8, 9, 12] is very complex.
Firstly, equations of this class, called subcritical, with mollified white noises, can
be lifted to equations in spaces of the so-called modelled distributions which are
functions with values in other spaces called model spaces of regularity structures.
The lifted equations can be solved and pushed down to equations in Besov spaces
using a reconstruction operator defined in terms of smooth models for regularity
structures. The smooth models depend on the mollification parameter ρ and one
shows that there is an appropriate choice of a smooth model such that when ρ →0
the pushed down solutions converge in a reasonable sense. However, the pushed
down “renormalized” equations, which are the original equations with mollified
noises and extra counter terms, often have no meaning in the limit ρ →0 since
coefficients in the counter terms may diverge when ρ →0.

Note that the method suggested in [8, 9, 12, 27] yields a local existence theorem
for the Cauchy problem, where t plays the role of time, similar to Picard’s theorem
for the local existence and uniqueness of solutions to ordinary differential equations.
This method was applied in [10] to equation (1.2.8) with the spatial part defined
on the three–dimensional torus (see also [11] for the case of the two–dimensional
torus). Note that m = 0 in this case. The corresponding counter term obtained
in [10, 11], that should be added to the right hand side of equation (1.2.8) with a
mollified white noise, has the form cA, where c is a constant which depends on ρ.
Note that the mollified noise ξρ is not manifestly gauge covariant. But the gauge
covariance can be recovered in the limit ρ →0, at least in law.
The renormalized equation (1.2.8) with a mollified white noise has the form
(1.2.11)
∂tA + m2A = −d∗
AFA −dAd∗A + cA + ξρ,
or, in terms of the coordinates,
(1.2.12) (∂t −∆+ m2)Ai = g[Aj, 2∂jAi −∂iAj + g[Aj, Ai]] + cAi + ξρ
i , i = 1, 2, 3.
In papers [4, 5, 35, 36, 37, 38, 39, 44] an alternative multi–index approach to regu-
larity structures and to their applications for solving non-linear parabolic stochastic
partial differential equations was developed. This approach has several benefits.
Firstly, the multi–index combinatorics is much simpler that the combinatorics
of colored threes used in [8, 9, 12]. Secondly, in the multi–index framework one
can obtain solutions to the corresponding stochastic differential equations using a
priori estimates and the continuity method (see [4]).
In the case of Yang-Mills measures Hairer’s approach yields local in time solutions
to the corresponding renormalized equation (1.2.11), with the spatial part defined
on the two–dimensional or the three–dimensional torus, and m = 0, as described
in [10] and [11].
In the two–dimensional case the Yang-Mills measure obtained
long time ago by other means (see [34, 42] or [20, 24] for earlier works) is an
invariant measure for the corresponding Markov process defined on a space of gauge
equivalence classes of connections (see [14]). But the construction of global solutions
to equation (1.2.11), and of the corresponding Yang-Mills measure in the three–
dimensional case are still cornerstone open problems.
The purpose of the project initiated in this paper is to develop the multi–index
approach for solving equation (1.2.11). In particular, in this paper we construct a
regularity structure associated to equation (1.2.11), prove the existence of a model
for it, and obtain stochastic and pointwise estimates for the model. For this purpose
we develop the multi–index approach for systems of equations and vector–valued
noises.
From now on we only consider equation (1.2.11) associated to the Yang-Mills
finctional on R3. We mainly follow the multi–index approach as it is presented in
[5], and refer the reader to [5] for motivations of the definitions given in this paper
for equation (1.2.11).
According to [5], the renormalization parameter c in equation (1.2.11) should be
a polynomial in the coupling constant g without constant term. The constant m is a
free parameter of the theory. As we mentioned in the first part of the Introduction,
for the existence of the Yang-Mills measure in the abelian case on R3 when k = R,
g = 0, and the polynomial c reduces to zero, m2 must be strictly positive (see also
[30], Example 1.3 and Remark 1.16 in [15]). Therefore we naturally assume that in


## Page 9


A TREE–FREE APPROACH TO 3D YANG–MILLS LANGEVIN DYNAMIC
9
the non–Abelien case m > 0 is a constant independent of ρ, and c is a polynomial
in g without constant term the coefficients of which depend on ρ.
Equation (1.2.12) is our main object of study.
Note that the operator L :=
∂t −∆+ m2, which appears in its right hand side, has much better properties than
the heat operator commonly used in both Hairer’s and the multi–index approaches.
In particular, at infinity its Green function behaves like a function from the Schwartz
space.
Note that m plays the role of a mass parameter in the theory, and it is expected
that the condition m > 0 ensures convergence of the corresponding Markov process
on a space of gauge equivalence classes of connections, existence of an invariant
Yang–Mills measure for it, and the spectral gap for its generator which should play
the role of the Hamiltonian of the canonically quantized Yang–Mills theory on the
Minkowski space as shown in [43]. The last fact explains the mass gap phenomenon
in quantum chromodynamics.
In conclusion we briefly describe the structure of the paper. More comments on
the content can be found in the beginning of each section.
In Section 2, after preliminary discussion of scaling properties of functions from
Besov spaces and of some properties of the operator L, we introduce the model space
and the smooth model for a regularity structure associated to equation (1.2.12).
We follow the philosophy of the multi–index approach in the form described in [5]
for scalar equations. But our definition of the model space adapted for systems
of equations with vector–valued noises requires vector–valued analogues of multi–
indices which appear in Section 2.3.
The definition of the recentered maps and of the canonical lift for a smooth
model of the regularity structure in Section 2.4 is given by applying a combination
of the original Hairer’s method from [27] and of the multi–index approach, the
combinatorics being close to the multi–index approach, and the analytic formulas
resemble Hairer’s definition based on the notion of regularizing kernels. The reason
for this combined approach originates in the fact that we prefer the simplified
multi–index combinatorics but consider the case when ξ is a white noise, which is
important for preserving gauge covariance, while in [5] the case of less rough noises
is considered. The last fact allows to introduce renentered maps using the heat
kernel itself, not its cut–off version, called a regularizing kernel, as in [29].
In turn, this possibility is based on the scaling properties of noises. White noises
scale (in law) with rational exponents under dilations on the underlying space R4
which a priori forces to use estimates for the regularizing kernel in H¨older spaces
with integer exponents in the theory of regularity structures. But actually such
estimates can only be obtained in Zygmund spaces which are different from H¨older
spaces at integer exponents.
One way to circumvent this obstruction is to manually distort the exponents in
the definition of regularity structures which is done in the original Hairer’s approach.
In the setting of this paper the need for such distortion is already clear from the
fact that the components of the white noise ξ belong to the spaces Bs
∞,∞(w) with
s < −5
2. This method is applicable in the case of white noises, while the other way
is to consider less rough noises which already scale in law with irrational exponents,
as it is done in [5].
Since gauge covariance, which is only preserved in the case of the white noise, is
primarily important for our purposes we follow the former path. Its disadvantage


## Page 10


10
A. SEVOSTYANOV
is that in [5] uniform estimates at all scales are obtained for the model, while in
the original Hairer’s approach estimates are local and the scaling parameter takes
values in a bounded interval, the last restriction being impossible to overcome in
the case of white noises.
Since we stick to Hairer’s definition of the recentered
maps in its analytic part, we still use a cut–off version of the kernel of the Green
function of the operator L in this definition. We already mentioned that this Green
function behaves like a Schwartz function at infinity unlike the heat kernel itself
which is even not integrable. Nevertheless, the Green function of L is not compactly
supported which makes it unsuitable for Hairer’s definition of recentered maps.
The structure group maps are defined in Section 2.6 with the help of automor-
phisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift which appear in Section
2.5. The definition of these automorphisms is simple and explicit. Note that the
multi–index description of the regularity structure is dual in a sense to the one
in Hairer’s approach, and the definition of automorphisms relating the recentered
maps and the canonical lift is obtained by “dualizing” a similar definition in terms
of regularity structures where it becomes inductive and rather inexplicit (see [27],
§8.2 or [16], Section 2). Formulas of the same type also appeared in [36, 37].
In Section 2.7 we also show, following Hairer’s approach, how to construct a
natural lift of equation (1.2.12) to an equation in a space of modelled distributions.
Section 3 contains analytic tools required for obtaining stochastic estimates for
models. After introducing some function spaces and recalling some classical Sobolev
type inequalities we state Schauder estimates for regularizing kernels and the re-
construction theorem for germs in Section 3.2. We need stochastic pointed and
local versions of the Schauder estimates which are close to those obtained in [6] in
the deterministic case (see also [22], Chapter 14). A stochastic version of Hairer’s
reconstruction theorem for germs is stated in a form close to Theorem 3.2 in [7]
(see also [13]).
The stochastic versions of these two statements are required in
the multi–index approach to stochastic estimates for models as suggested in [5, 35]
where their global stochastic versions are used. Section 3.3 also contains necessar-
ily results on Malliavin derivatives of the recentered maps used in the proof of the
stochastic estimates in the multi–index approach.
Section 4 is central in this paper. After recalling in Section 4.1 the so–called gen-
eralized BPHZ condition used to fix the renormalization parameter c(ρ) we confirm
the translational invariance in law for the recentered and for the structure group
maps in Section 4.2 and proceed with the statement of the stochastic estimates for
them in Section 4.3.
The proof of these estimates presented in Sections 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.7, 4.8 and 4.9
is quite lengthy. We proceed by induction over the indices from the index set of the
regularity structure following the idea of [5, 35]. However, in contrast to [5, 35], we
linearly order the arguments in the proof. The other difference is that, since we use
local and pointed Schauder estimates for a regularizing kernel on a bounded scale,
which naturally appears in a definition of the relevant H¨older spaces, the range of the
scaling parameter shrinks after each iteration of the induction step, and to run the
induction step again this parameter must be greater than one. As a consequence,
starting from a large enough range for the rescaling parameter in the base case, we
can obtain stochastic estimates for recentered maps and for structure group maps
labeled by indices from the index set of the regularity structure uniformly bounded
by a given constant which can be chosen arbitrary large. The BPHZ conditions in


## Page 11


A TREE–FREE APPROACH TO 3D YANG–MILLS LANGEVIN DYNAMIC
11
our approach should also be fixed at each iteration of the induction step separately
for recentered maps labeled by the corresponding indices from the index set.
In Section 5 we show that the smooth models converge in a certain space when
the mollification of the white noise is removed, i.e. when ρ →0. The proof is based,
as in [25, 44], on a Cauchy property for smooth models which is established in the
same manner as the stochastic estimates.
Finally, in Section 6 using Kolmogorov continuity arguments we obtain weighted
Besov type global pointwise estimates for models which hold almost surely. This is
done in Theorem 6.2.1 for the structure group maps of the model and in Theorem
6.3.1 for the recentered maps.
Acknowledgements. The author is grateful to Thierry L´evy and to Ilya Chevyrev
for stimulating discussions at the initial stage of this project.
2. A regularity structure and a model for the Langevin equation
with mollified noise
In this section we introduce a regularity structure and a smooth model for it
associated to equation (1.2.12). We start by defining the underlying combinatorics
relying on the notion of multi–indices which appear in Section 2.1. In the defini-
tion of the smooth model for the regularity structure we explicitly use the kernel
of the heat operator with an extra mass term the properties of which are recalled
in Section 2.2. Then we proceed with an algebraic description of the model space
in Section 2.3. Using this model space recentered maps of a smooth model for the
corresponding regularity structure and a canonical lift for it associated to equation
(1.2.12) are defined in Section 2.4. In the algebraic part of their definition in Lemma
2.4.1 we follow the strategy of [5]. But, as we mentioned in the introduction, ana-
lytic formulas (2.4.9) for the recentered maps have some similarity with the original
Hairer’s definition given in [30] as well as provisional local analytic estimates for
them in the end of Section 2.4. The structure group maps of the smooth model are
defined with the help of model space automorphisms relating the recentered maps
and the canonical lift as in the original Hairer’s approach. However, we introduce
these automorphisms in Section 2.5 using the multi–index combinatorics in terms
of which their definitions are much more simple and explicit. The definition of the
structure group automorphisms resembles Hairer’s definition and their properties
derived in Lemma 2.6.1 are similar to those obtained in [5] in the case of a sin-
gle scalar equation. In the end of this section we explicitly relate the constructed
smooth model to equation (1.2.12). To this end we define in Section 2.7 a lift of
equation (1.2.12) to an equation for modelled distributions and show that after
applying the so-called reconstruction operator this equation is reduced to equation
(1.2.12).

Lemma 2.3.1. The subset H of R of the homogeneities |β| of all possible multi–
indices β and the set I := {|β| : β ∈M} ⊂H ⊂R are subsets of the set (α +
1)N + N −αN + α. In particular, H and I are locally finite, i.e. any bounded
open subinterval of R contains finite numbers of elements of both H and of I. α
is the minimal element for both H and I. In particular, for any r ∈R the set
{β ∈Mult : |β| ≤r} is finite. Moreover, β = 0 is the only multi–index for which
|β| = α.
Proof. Formula (2.1.5) implies that the set H is a subset of the set (α + 1)N + N −
αN + α. Since α + 1 and −α are strictly positive, we deduce from formula (2.1.5)
that α is the minimal element of H and of I, and β = 0 is the only multi–index for
which |β| = α.
For the same reason for any r ∈R the set ((α + 1)N + N −αN + α) ∩(−∞, r)
is finite. Since H ⊂(α + 1)N + N −αN + α, we infer that H is locally finite, and
hence I ⊂H is locally finite as well.
□

2.5. Automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift.
In this section, as a technical tool for defining structure group maps we introduce
certain automorphisms relating the recentered maps and the canonical lift. For this
purpose we consider a formal power series algebra in terms of which these auto-
morphisms are defined. Firstly, we introduce the relevant notions and terminology.

In [5, 35] arguments based on formulas (2.6.16) and (2.6.22) are called the al-
gebraic and the three–point argument, respectively. They are common technical
proof tools in the multi–index approach to regularity structures.
2.7. Modelled distributions and the lift of the Langevin equation to an
equation for modelled distributions. Estimates (2.6.13) are similar to those
obtained in Proposition 8.27 in [27]. Together with parts (i), (iii), (vii) of Propo-
sition 2.6.1, (2.4.8), (2.4.10), (2.4.11) and Proposition (2.4.2) they imply that
(Πx, ΓT
xy) is a smooth model for the regularity structure T in the sense of Hairer
(see [27], Definition 2.17).
Estimates (2.4.22), (2.4.23) and (2.6.13) are rather provisional as well as the
definition of modelled distributions given in this section. Actually in Section 4 we
shall obtain stochastic estimates which hold uniformly for all ρ > 0, and in Section
5 we show that the models (Πx, ΓT
xy) converge in a certain sense when ρ →0 in
such a way that the estimates still hold after taking the limit. The proper definition
of modelled distributions should be then given using the limit of the sequence of
the smooth models for which global pointwise estimates similar to (2.4.22), (2.4.23)
and (2.6.13) are obtained in Section 6.
The purpose of this section is just to show that the lift of equation (1.2.12)
to an equation for modelled distributions is reduced to the original equation after
applying the so-called reconstruction operator (see Theorem 3.10 in [27] for its
definition).

Equation (2.7.15) resembles equation (7.18) in [27]. We claim that solutions to
equation (2.7.15) satisfy (1.2.12).
In this section we do not discuss the existence of solutions to equation (2.7.15).
Actually one can show that the right hand side of (2.7.15) belongs to Dκ(T). We do
not prove this result as we shall not need it in this paper. We only note that, for any

3. Analytic tools
In this section we recall some analytic results required for the proof of stochastic
estimates for a model in Section 4.
In Section 3.3 we also obtain some results
related to Malliavin (directional) derivatives for recentered maps used in this proof.

We shall also need the so-called spectral gap inequality in the case when the
underlying probability space is the white noise space modeled on S′ ⊗V , where V
is a Euclidean space, with the covariance induced by the scalar product on V , so
that its Cameron–Martin space CM is L2(R4) ⊗V with the natural induced scalar
product. In fact, in our case V = k ⊗R3 where the scalar product is induced by
−( · , · ) on k, and by the standard Euclidean scalar product on R3 fixed in the
Introduction. Denote also by WN the corresponding space of S′⊗V –valued random
variables. Note that the white noise space variable ξ can be regarded as an element
of WN.

3.2. Schauder estimates and reconstruction theorem for germs. Following
the strategy of [5, 35] for establishing stochastic estimates for models we shall use
stochastic versions of Schauder estimates for the operator K acting on germs which
are similar to those presented in [6] in the deterministic case (see also [22], Chapter
14). These estimates are closely related to multilevel Schauder estimates which
initially appeared in [30].
Another important ingredient used in [5, 35] is a stochastic version of the re-
construction theorem for germs. We state and use it in a form similar to Theorem
3.2 in [7]. This theorem is, in turn, a generalization of the reconstruction theorem
suggested in [30] for modelled distributions (see also [13, 26] for other versions of
the reconstruction theorem).
Note that a major difference between the Schauder estimates and the recon-
struction theorem used in [5] and analogous statements in this section is that in the
latter case the rescaling parameter, which naturally appears in both cases, takes
values in a bounded interval (compare with the discussion in Section 1.2 of the
scaling properties of the white noise and of less rough noises utilized in [5]).
Recall that a p–germ, 1 ≤p ≤∞, is a family F = (Fx)x∈R4 of E–valued random
distributions Fx such that for any φ ∈D the map
x 7→∥Fx(φ)∥Lp
is measurable.
If the random distributions Fx are smooth, we call the germ F smooth.

4. Stochastic estimates for the model
In this section we state and prove the main result of this paper, stochastic esti-
mates for the smooth models (Πx, ΓT
xy) constructed in Section 2. The proof is quite
complicated but follows the proof of a similar statement in [5] with some modifi-
cations. As we mentioned in the Introduction, the main difference comparing to
[5] is that we obtain the stochastic estimates in a bounded range of the rescaling
parameter which is one of the ingredients of them. This leads to some modification
of the BPHZ condition imposed on the model in the course of the proof. The argu-
ments in all statements related to estimates involving the BPHZ condition are also
adjusted accordingly. In fact, these arguments are more close to the ones which
appear in the proof of Proposition 5.2 in [25].
After recalling the BPHZ condition in Section 4.1 and the translational invariance
in law property for models in Section 4.2 we state the main result in Section 4.3
and then proceed with its proof.
4.1. A generalized BPHZ condition. To obtain stochastic estimates stable un-
der the limit ρ →0 for the smooth model introduced in the previous section one
has to fix the coefficients ck, k = 1, 2, 3, 4 in (2.4.5) in an appropriate way. This is
achieved by imposing the so-called BPHZ condition. We say that a model (Πx, ΓT
xy)
satisfies a generalized BPHZ condition with respect to a family of functions ψβ ∈S,
β ∈M′, |β| < 2 if for all β ∈M′, |β| < 2 one has
(4.1.1)
EΠ−
0β(ψβ) = 0,
where
Π−
0β(ψβ) =
Z
R4 Π−
0β(x)ψβ(x)dx.
For any given β ∈M′ with |β| < 2 we also say that Π−
0β satisfies the generalized
BPHZ condition with respect to ψβ if (4.1.1) holds.
Proposition 4.1.1. For any family of functions ψβ ∈S, β ∈M′, |β| < 2, which
are even in the variables xi, i = 1, 2, 3, there is a unique model of the form (Πx, ΓT
xy)
which satisfies (4.1.1).
In fact, the unique model is completely determined by ψβ for β = kδg + δ0,
k = 1, 2, 3, 4, and for the other β ∈M′, |β| < 2 the corresponding generalized
BPHZ conditions are satisfied automatically.

Strategy of the proof of Proposition 4.5.11. We outline the strategy of the proof
which will be presented as a series of statements in Sections 4.6, 4.7, 4.8 and 4.9.
The conditions in each statement in these sections depend on the results obtained
in the preceding statements only and on the induction assumptions imposed in
this proposition. However, claims in Sections 4.6, 4.7, 4.8 and 4.9 are often stated
under weaker assumptions and sometimes results stronger than those required for
the induction step are proved.
Estimates (4.5.14), (4.5.17) and (4.5.18) for ¯λ≺replaced by ¯λ, µ = β and γ ∈
M \ Mpp = M′ are established in Propositions 4.6.1, 4.6.2 and 4.6.3, respectively,
(see also Corollary 4.6.4 for the exact statement), and for µ = β and γ ∈Mpp they
are obtained in Propositions 4.9.3, 4.9.7, and 4.9.8, respectively (see Corollaries
4.9.4 and 4.9.9 for the exact statement).
Estimate (4.5.15) for ¯λ≺replaced by ¯λ, µ = β follows from Lemma 4.8.2, Corol-
lary 4.8.4 and from Proposition 4.8.5 for |β| < 2, and it is derived in Proposition
4.8.6 for |β| > 2 (see Corollary 4.8.7 for the exact statement).
Estimate (4.5.16) for ¯λ≺replaced by ¯λ, µ = β is obtained in Proposition 4.9.5
(see Corollary 4.9.6 for the exact statement).

5. Convergence of models
In this section we show that the smooth models (Πx(ρ), ΓT
xy(ρ)) converge in a
certain sense when ρ →0 and the mollification is removed.
The proof of this
statement is based on a Cauchy property for models which we state in the next
section.
5.1. The Cauchy property for models. The proof of the Cauchy property for
models closely follows the same steps as the proof of Theorem 4.3.1. We begin by
stating this property in this section.
For any function F(ρ) of a real variable ρ > 0 we denote ∆ρ,ρ′F := F(ρ)−F(ρ′).
In the statement below we keep the notation of Theorem 4.3.1.

following simple observation. All estimates, arguments and identities which appear

∆ρ,ρ′ to their right hand sides and left hand sides using the following formal rules.

where the meaning of the product F1 . . . Fn depends on the context. E.g., for real

So
to estimate differences of products of such functions at different points ρ and ρ′
one should use the same telescoping trick as in the proof of the product rule for

hand side, as by the induction assumption only estimates of differences ∆ρ,ρ′(Fi)
can give factors (max{ρ, ρ′})ε.

Proof of Theorem 5.1.1. Firstly, as in the proof of Theorem 4.3.1 note that by
Lemma 2.6.1 (iv) and (v) estimate (5.3.17) holds for all µ ∈Mpp and for all
µ ∈M′ \M≥0 with any ¯λ≺, and by (2.4.8), (2.4.11), (2.4.10), (2.4.5), Lemma 2.4.1
(iii), (2.4.23) estimates (5.3.18), (5.3.19) also hold for all µ ̸∈M≥0 and for any ¯λ≺.
Actually in all cases the left hand sides in (5.3.17), (5.3.18) and (5.3.19) are equal
to zero as the expressions in the left hand sides of (4.5.14), (4.5.15), (4.5.16) do not
depend on ρ.
By (3.3.19) and (3.3.23) estimates (5.3.20) and (5.3.21) trivially hold for all
µ ̸∈M≥0 and for any ¯λ≺.
Therefore it suffices to establish estimates (5.1.1), (5.1.2) and (5.1.3) for the
model defined in Theorem 4.3.1, and for µ ∈M≥0, since for µ ̸∈M≥0 the left
hand sides of these inequalities are equal to zero.
We can also assume in the
proof that estimates (5.3.17), (5.3.18), (5.3.19), (5.3.20) and (5.3.21) hold for all
µ ∈M \ M≥0 and for any ¯λ≺.
We claim that for the model defined in Theorem 4.3.1 estimates (5.1.1), (5.1.2)
and (5.1.3) hold for µ ∈M≥0, |µ|≺< N. The proof, which is similar to that of
Theorem 4.3.1, is by induction over the modified homogeneity.
Firstly, recall that µ = 0 is the only element of M≥0 with minimal possible
modified homogeneity, |0|≺= 2 −ε.
For µ = 0 and arbitrary ¯λ≺= ¯λ0 > 0
estimates (5.3.17), (5.3.20), (5.3.21), (5.3.18) and (5.3.19) follow from the results of
Sections 4.4 and 5.2, see Proposition 4.4.1 for (5.3.17), Propositions 4.4.2 and 5.2.7
for (5.3.20), Propositions 4.4.3 and 5.2.8 for (5.3.21), Proposition 5.2.5 for (5.3.18),

and Proposition 5.2.6 for (5.3.19). Therefore for β ∈M≥0, such that β ̸= 0 and
|β|≺is minimal possible, the induction assumptions are satisfied, and we can begin
the induction procedure over the modified homogeneity the induction step of which
is established in Proposition 5.3.9. Conditions (4.5.21) ensure that the generalized
BPHZ conditions used in the induction procedure are satisfied.
Note that by the condition ¯λM(N) ≥1, where M is the same as in the proof of
Theorem 4.3.1, the induction step can be iterated until we exhaust the set of all
multi-indices β ∈M≥0 with |β|≺< N, and after completing the last iteration we
obtain estimates (5.1.1), (5.1.2) and (5.1.3) for all µ ∈M≥0 with |µ|≺< N and
¯λ = ¯λM(N). This completes the proof.
□

Equip MdN ¯λ0 with a topology defined by the family of pseudo–distances dNp, 1 ≤
p < ∞, where a sequence of models in MdN ¯λ0 converges if it converges with respect
to all pseudo–distances dNp, 1 ≤p < ∞. Denote by MdN ¯λ0 the corresponding
topological space obtained by the metric identification. Its topology is defined by
the family of metrics corresponding to dNp, 1 ≤p < ∞. We denote these new
metrics by the same symbols. When we speak about a model in the space MdN ¯λ0
we mean its equivalence class in MdN ¯λ0.
Note that the models in Theorems 4.3.1, 5.1.1 belong to MdN ¯λ0, where N ≥17.
This follows from the estimates obtained in Theorem 4.3.1, from the definition
of the recentered maps in Section 2.4 (see especially properties (2.4.8), (2.4.10),
(2.4.11) and Lemma 2.4.1(iii)), from the properties of the structure group maps
stated in Proposition 2.6.1 (i)-(v), Lemma 2.6.2 and from (4.2.1), (4.2.2).
Now from Theorem 5.1.1 using the Cauchy criterion and from formulas (2.4.4)
we immediately obtain the following result
Theorem 5.4.3. For N ≥17 the models (Π(ρ), ΓT (ρ)) ∈MdN ¯λ0, ρ > 0 defined
in Theorem 4.3.1 converge to a model (Π, ΓT ) ∈MdN ¯λ0 when ρ →0. This model,
called a model of level N at scale ¯λ0 for the regularity structure with model space
T associated to equation (1.2.8), also satisfies Π−

6. Pointwise estimates for models
In this section we show that the recentered maps and the structure group maps
for models of level N at scale ¯λ0 introduced in the previous section almost surely
satisfy pointwise estimates similar to (5.4.6), (5.4.11) and (5.4.12).

and (6.2.1) may depend on ρ only via (5.4.6), (5.4.7) and (5.4.8), we obtain the
following corollary.
Corollary 6.3.2. The recentered maps and the structure group maps of the smooth
model of level N at scale ¯λ0 for the regularity structure with model space T defined
in Theorem 4.3.1 satisfy estimates (6.3.1), (6.3.2) and (6.2.1) with arbitrary weight
w, and the constants in inequalities (6.3.1), (6.3.2) and (6.2.1) in this case do not
depend on the mollification parameter ρ.
As a consequence of Theorem 5.4.3 and of the definition of models of level N we
also obtain another corollary.
Corollary 6.3.3. The recentered maps and the structure group maps of the model
of level N at scale ¯λ0 for the regularity structure with model space T associated to
equation (1.2.8) and defined in Theorem 5.4.3 satisfy estimates (6.3.1), (6.3.2) and
(6.2.1) with arbitrary weight w.
The last two corollaries, arguments similar to those presented before Corollary
6.3.2 in conjunction with Theorems 5.1.1, 5.4.3 and with the usual telescoping
trick as in the proof of Proposition 5.3.9 yield the following statement which is a
pointwise counterpart of Theorem 5.4.3.
Corollary 6.3.4. Let Πxµ(ρ), Π−

ture group maps of the smooth model of level N at scale ¯λ0 satisfying ¯λM(N) ≥8
for the regularity structure with model space T defined in Theorem 4.3.1, and Πxµ,
Π−

at the scale ¯λ0 for the regularity structure with model space T associated to equation
(1.2.8) and defined in Theorem 5.4.3. Then for any weight w and r > 2 −α−we
